### Modular Machine Learning: An Indispensable Path towards New-Generation Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20020v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Wang, Haoyang Li, Zeyang Zhang, Haibo Chen, Wenwu Zhu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have dramatically advanced machine learning research including natural language processing, computer vision, data mining, etc., yet they still exhibit critical limitations in reasoning, factual consistency, and interpretability. In this paper, we introduce a novel learning paradigm -- Modular Machine Learning (MML) -- as an essential approach toward new-generation LLMs. MML decomposes the complex structure of LLMs into three interdependent components: modular representation, modular model, and modular reasoning, aiming to enhance LLMs' capability of counterfactual reasoning, mitigating hallucinations, as well as promoting fairness, safety, and transparency. Specifically, the proposed MML paradigm can: i) clarify the internal working mechanism of LLMs through the disentanglement of semantic components; ii) allow for flexible and task-adaptive model design; iii) enable interpretable and logic-driven decision-making process. We present a feasible implementation of MML-based LLMs via leveraging advanced techniques such as disentangled representation learning, neural architecture search and neuro-symbolic learning. We critically identify key challenges, such as the integration of continuous neural and discrete symbolic processes, joint optimization, and computational scalability, present promising future research directions that deserve further exploration. Ultimately, the integration of the MML paradigm with LLMs has the potential to bridge the gap between statistical (deep) learning and formal (logical) reasoning, thereby paving the way for robust, adaptable, and trustworthy AI systems across a wide range of real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary**

The paper introduces "Modular Machine Learning" (MML) as a new paradigm for developing next-generation Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is to decompose LLMs into three interconnected modules: modular representation, modular model, and modular reasoning. This decomposition aims to improve LLMs' reasoning abilities, factual consistency, interpretability, fairness, and safety. The paper suggests using techniques such as disentangled representation learning, neural architecture search, and neuro-symbolic learning to implement MML. It discusses the key benefits of MML in enabling counterfactual reasoning, rule-based reasoning, unification of perception and reasoning, and hallucination mitigation. The paper also identifies challenges and future research directions, including the seamless integration of neural and symbolic components, joint optimization, scalability, and evaluation methodologies. Finally, it presents an example implementation of the MML framework for Visual Question Answering (VQA).

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The idea of modularizing LLMs is not entirely new. There have been prior efforts in incorporating external tools, knowledge bases, and rule-based systems to augment LLMs. However, the proposed MML framework provides a specific structure (modular representation, model, and reasoning) and combines several advanced techniques (DRL, NAS, NSL) in a holistic manner. The combination and structured approach offer novelty, even if individual components have been explored previously. The paper's definition of MML and its proposed implementation for VQA also show a clear demonstration of the concepts in practice. The joint optimization approach, though not detailed deeply, points to a novel direction of training.

**Significance:** The issues addressed by MML are undeniably significant. Reasoning, factual consistency, interpretability, and safety are critical limitations hindering the wider adoption of LLMs, especially in high-stakes applications. MML addresses them through modularity. Enhancing these aspects would greatly improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs. The modular approach is promising for tackling issues like bias and hallucinations, which significantly harm LLMs, and for creating LLMs that are more faithful to rules and knowledge. The detailed discussions of different techniques (DRL, NAS, NSL) and their applications for modular components provide practical implications for the research community. The identified challenges provide a valuable roadmap for future research. However, the paper only provides conceptual ideas and a limited instantiation with VQA.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Rigorous Empirical Validation:** The primary weakness of the paper is the lack of substantial empirical results. While the VQA example provides an initial proof-of-concept, there are no quantitative evaluations demonstrating the effectiveness of the MML framework compared to existing LLMs on various benchmark datasets.
*   **Implementation Details:** The descriptions of DRL, NAS, and NSL integration, while informative, lack concrete technical details. The paper needs a detailed algorithm for the joint optimization. The implementation could have been fleshed out further to make it more reproducible or immediately implementable for other researchers.
*   **Limited Scope:** The paper primarily focuses on conceptual ideas and general strategies. A more focused investigation of specific MML implementations for a few key LLM applications would have added more impact.
*   **Over-Claiming:** While the paper addresses critical LLM issues, some statements might be slightly over-claimed. The paper presents the MML approach as the *indispensable path* to new-generation LLMs. It might be better to acknowledge that MML is one *potential* pathway, along with other alternative approaches.

**Score Justification:**

Based on the above evaluation, the paper introduces valuable ideas and a structured approach to address significant challenges in LLMs, but its impact is limited by the lack of empirical validation and implementation details.

Score: 6.5

**Rationale:**

*   **+1.5:** Novel combination of techniques (DRL, NAS, NSL) within a structured MML framework. Definition of the MML framework gives a clearer demonstration of the concepts in practice.
*   **+1.5:** Addresses significant challenges in LLMs (reasoning, consistency, interpretability, safety, fairness).
*   **+1.0:** Identifies valuable future research directions and potential benefits of MML.
*   **-1.0:** Lacks rigorous empirical validation and substantial quantitative results.
*   **-1.0:** Implementation details are limited, reducing immediate practical impact.
*   **-0.5:** Scope is broad, lacks in-depth investigation of specific MML implementations.

Therefore, I assign a score of **6.5**, which signifies that the paper presents a worthwhile contribution with promising potential but requires substantial empirical support and technical details to solidify its position in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Better To Ask in English? Evaluating Factual Accuracy of Multilingual LLMs in English and Low-Resource Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20022v1)
- **Authors**: Pritika Rohera, Chaitrali Ginimav, Gayatri Sawant, Raviraj Joshi
- **Abstract**: Multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated significant effectiveness across various languages, particularly in high-resource languages such as English. However, their performance in terms of factual accuracy across other low-resource languages, especially Indic languages, remains an area of investigation. In this study, we assess the factual accuracy of LLMs - GPT-4o, Gemma-2-9B, Gemma-2-2B, and Llama-3.1-8B - by comparing their performance in English and Indic languages using the IndicQuest dataset, which contains question-answer pairs in English and 19 Indic languages. By asking the same questions in English and their respective Indic translations, we analyze whether the models are more reliable for regional context questions in Indic languages or when operating in English. Our findings reveal that LLMs often perform better in English, even for questions rooted in Indic contexts. Notably, we observe a higher tendency for hallucination in responses generated in low-resource Indic languages, highlighting challenges in the multilingual understanding capabilities of current LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the factual accuracy of multilingual large language models (LLMs) in English and 19 low-resource Indic languages. It uses the IndicQuest dataset to compare LLM performance on the same questions translated into English and Indic languages. The study evaluates several prominent LLMs, including GPT-4o, Gemma-2-9B, Gemma-2-2B, and Llama-3.1-8B.  The key findings are that LLMs often perform better on questions in English, even when those questions are rooted in Indic contexts; that hallucination is more frequent in responses generated in low-resource Indic languages; and that the size of the model doesn't uniformly improve performance across all languages. It further notes that languages like Odia and Urdu show the lowest scores. Additionally, the domain influences the accuracy, with History and Geography showcasing the lowest scores.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Focus on a Significant Problem:** Addressing the factual accuracy of LLMs in low-resource languages, particularly Indic languages, is highly relevant. The performance disparity between high- and low-resource languages is a known problem, but the work provides a detailed analysis focusing specifically on the understudied Indic language group.
    *   **Use of a Specific Dataset:** Utilizing the IndicQuest dataset allows for controlled comparison, ensuring semantic consistency across languages. This is a significant strength as it mitigates potential biases introduced by varying question content.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper assesses multiple LLMs and uses a range of evaluation metrics (factual accuracy, relevance, clarity, language consistency, conciseness, and overall score), providing a multifaceted view of performance.
    *   **Clear Findings:** The results are clearly presented and support the conclusions drawn. The paper identifies specific areas of weakness (e.g., certain domains and languages) for LLMs.
    *   **Emphasis on Hallucination:** The paper highlights the concerning problem of hallucination in low-resource languages.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Judge Limitation:** The evaluation relies on another LLM (Llama-3.1-405b) as a judge. While this is a common practice, it introduces potential biases and limitations. The judge's own factual accuracy and understanding of nuanced regional contexts in each language are critical factors. The paper should acknowledge this more explicitly.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed qualitative error analysis, categorizing the types of factual inaccuracies that occur. This would provide more actionable insights for improving LLM performance. While the paper provides some hallucination examples in Figure 5, it lacks an analysis of common error patterns.
    *   **Limited Scope of Solutions:** While the paper effectively highlights the problem, it lacks a detailed discussion of potential solutions. It briefly mentions enriching datasets and domain-specific optimizations, but these ideas need to be expanded upon. The paper could discuss data augmentation, fine-tuning strategies, or knowledge injection techniques specific to Indic languages.
    *   **Novelty is Limited:** The problem of factual inaccuracy in low-resource languages is well known and, while there are merits to its targeted focus on Indic languages, the overall methodology is straightforward.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper provides a useful evaluation of existing LLMs on Indic languages, a relatively underexplored area. However, the methodology is primarily an application of existing techniques to a new dataset and language group. The main contribution lies in quantifying the performance disparity and highlighting the challenges specific to Indic languages. The findings are valuable for researchers working on multilingual LLMs, but the lack of methodological innovation limits the overall significance.
*   **Potential influence:** The paper's potential impact stems from its contribution to quantifying LLM performance in Indic languages, which will encourage further work. By making the IndicQuest dataset more broadly known, the paper may influence the direction of future research in this area.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper provides a valuable evaluation of LLM performance on a specific set of languages (Indic) and domain (regional context). The use of the IndicQuest dataset is a strength. However, the evaluation methodology is relatively standard, and the paper lacks significant methodological innovation or detailed error analysis. The limited discussion of potential solutions also detracts from the overall impact. The main strength is that it validates and quantifies the existing assumptions that multilingual LLMs perform badly in low-resource languages, which encourages future work and validates investment.

### SpatialReasoner: Towards Explicit and Generalizable 3D Spatial Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20024v1)
- **Authors**: Wufei Ma, Yu-Cheng Chou, Qihao Liu, Xingrui Wang, Celso de Melo, Jieneng Chen, Jianwen Xie, Alan Yuille
- **Abstract**: Recent studies in 3D spatial reasoning explore data-driven approaches and achieve enhanced spatial reasoning performance with reinforcement learning (RL). However, these methods typically perform spatial reasoning in an implicit manner, and it remains underexplored whether the acquired 3D knowledge generalizes to unseen question types at any stage of the training. In this work we introduce SpatialReasoner, a novel large vision-language model (LVLM) that address 3D spatial reasoning with explicit 3D representations shared between stages -- 3D perception, computation, and reasoning. Explicit 3D representations provide a coherent interface that supports advanced 3D spatial reasoning and enable us to study the factual errors made by LVLMs. Results show that our SpatialReasoner achieve improved performance on a variety of spatial reasoning benchmarks and generalizes better when evaluating on novel 3D spatial reasoning questions. Our study bridges the 3D parsing capabilities of prior visual foundation models with the powerful reasoning abilities of large language models, opening new directions for 3D spatial reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SpatialReasoner: Towards Explicit and Generalizable 3D Spatial Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SpatialReasoner, a novel vision-language model (LVLM) designed for 3D spatial reasoning. Unlike many existing approaches that perform reasoning implicitly, SpatialReasoner utilizes explicit 3D representations at each stage: perception, computation, and reasoning. This allows the model to better understand and generalize to unseen question types. The approach involves a two-stage training process: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to learn explicit 3D representations, followed by reinforcement learning (RL) to enhance robustness and generalization. The authors extend an existing 3D pseudo-annotation pipeline to generate training data and experiment with various LVLMs, data combinations, and training methods. The results show that SpatialReasoner improves performance on spatial reasoning benchmarks, generalizes better to new question types, and allows for analyzing failure modes of LVLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the explicit use of 3D representations throughout the entire pipeline, from perception to reasoning. While prior works have explored 3D-aware LVLMs, the authors demonstrate a more coherent and interpretable approach. The two-stage training (SFT+RL) is also a significant contribution. The explicit separation and integration of 3D perception, computation, and reasoning is relatively novel and helps with interpretability.
*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses a fundamental limitation of existing LVLMs: their implicit and often brittle approach to 3D spatial reasoning. The explicit representation enables not only improved performance but also a better understanding of model behavior and failure modes. The authors also point to improvements in the compositional reasoning and extensibility to related tasks. The work suggests the integration of prior visual parsing with powerful reasoning capabilities, opening avenues for 3D spatial reasoning.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.
    *   The explicit 3D representation strategy allows for interpretability and compositional reasoning.
    *   Thorough experimentation with various LVLMs, data combinations, and training methods.
    *   Demonstrated improved performance and generalization on various benchmarks.
    *   Analysis of failure modes provides insights into model behavior.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The pipeline still relies on pseudo-annotations, and while the authors address this with human verification, the reliance on synthetic data remains a limitation. A purely trained on real-world data would be more convincing.
    *   The reliance on 2D shortcuts of the CVBench-3D benchmark.
    *   Some improvements are marginal, suggesting that further work may be needed to realize the full potential of the approach.
    *   The experiments seem to indicate that SFT is effective for a scalable approach to noisy data.
    *   The ablation study reveals that models can acquire a structured, multistep understanding without direct supervision of structured datasets.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work can influence future research in 3D-aware LVLMs by providing a more structured and interpretable approach. The explicit representation strategy can be adopted by others to improve performance and analyze model behavior. The insights into the trade-offs between SFT and RL can also inform the design of future training pipelines.
*   **Justification:** Overall, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of 3D spatial reasoning by introducing a novel and interpretable approach. The experimental results are convincing, and the analysis of failure modes provides valuable insights. While there are some limitations, the paper represents a significant step forward in addressing the challenges of 3D spatial reasoning with LVLMs.
*    **Discussion:** The authors effectively utilize explicit 3D representation in their pipeline, which results in improvements in performance metrics. What's more impressive is the degree of interpretability that these strategies introduce in otherwise opaque decision making processes. Future work on extending this technique to other areas of geometric reasoning is warranted and a high priority for future research.

**Score: 7.5**
